resolution: closed by engineering decision 2026-08-14: DW-6, DW-38, DW-45, DW-64 and DW-86 are the same review-budget entry filed against five stories. DW-6 carries the single combined follow-up review over stories 3, 6, 8, 9 and 10; nothing separate is owed here.

### DW-39: `keybinder_bind`'s discarded result makes AD-10's "a failed grab must not report success" unsatisfiable through `hotkey_manager`

origin: story 7-x11-global-hotkey-adapter.md, 2026-08-08
location: `hotkey_manager_linux-0.2.0/linux/hotkey_manager_linux_plugin.cc:95-99`; consumed by lib/src/infrastructure/hotkey/hotkey_manager_registrar.dart
severity: medium
reason: `hkm_register` calls `keybinder_bind(keystring, handle_key_down, NULL)` and **throws away its `gboolean`**, then answers `fl_method_success_response_new(fl_value_new_bool(true))` unconditionally. No Dart code can tell a taken grab from a refused one. The commonest real failure — another client already owns the combination — therefore arrives at `X11GlobalHotkey` as success, so `bind()` returns `HotkeyBound`, the tray states that hotkeys are available, and story 10's settings screen will show a shortcut that does nothing. This is the same class of defect story 6 found in `tray_manager`'s `set_icon`, on a load-bearing AD, and it is a property of the pinned package rather than of this code: nothing in Dart can observe the discarded return.
  **Proposed replacement, stated rather than built.** `dart:ffi` over `libkeybinder-3.0.so.0` closes this and DW-40 together: `DynamicLibrary.open` at bind time makes a missing library a caught error and an honest `HotkeyUnavailable`, calling `keybinder_bind` directly makes its result readable, and the accelerator string then genuinely is built in Dart, which is what AD-9 describes (see DW-41). Under AD-9 and this story's seam that is **one file** — `hotkey_manager_registrar.dart` swapped for a `keybinder_registrar.dart`, with `X11GlobalHotkey` and everything above it untouched. Its own risk is why it was not built here: keybinder calls GDK and installs an X11 event filter, so it must run on the GTK main thread, while Dart FFI calls run on the Flutter UI thread. Doing it safely means marshalling `keybinder_init`/`keybinder_bind` onto the main loop with `g_idle_add` and returning the result through a `NativeCallable.listener` — a large threading surface that **no test in this container could touch**, since there is no X display here at all. Owed to a session with a real desktop.
status: done 2026-09-02
resolution: 2026-09-02 Closed by plan 01-02 (`6abf480`), and **the route taken is not the route this entry's own `decision:` line one line below names.** That 2026-08-14 answer ratified a `dart:ffi` *keybinder* registrar — `keybinder_registrar.dart` opening `libkeybinder-3.0.so.0`, marshalling `keybinder_init`/`keybinder_bind` onto the GTK main loop with `g_idle_add` and answering through a `NativeCallable.listener`. What shipped is a `dart:ffi` **X11 passive-grab** registrar, `lib/src/infrastructure/hotkey/x11_key_grab_registrar.dart`, over `libX11.so.6` on a private `Display` in a worker isolate. **Both records stand; the 2026-09-01 route is the operative one, and the older `decision:` line is preserved verbatim and not rewritten.** Two reasons, so the supersession is not a preference: (a) *the marshalling that decision sketched does not compose.* A `NativeCallable` cannot serve as a GLib `GSourceFunc` — it neither returns a value to the caller nor runs on the calling thread — so `g_idle_add`'s contract (a callback returning `gboolean`, invoked on the loop's own thread) cannot be met by the mechanism named; the shipped registrar therefore uses `Pointer.fromFunction` for its X error trap instead, which Xlib invokes synchronously on the calling thread and which returns an `int` (`.planning/phases/01-hotkey-truth/01-02-SUMMARY.md`, key-decisions). (b) *the X11 route adds no runtime dependency, where the keybinder route keeps one.* `libX11.so.6` is already an unconditional `DT_NEEDED` of `libgdk-3.so.0` (`readelf -d /usr/lib/x86_64-linux-gnu/libgdk-3.so.0`, measured 2026-09-01), so on any host where this Flutter app starts at all the library is already resolved — which makes DW-40's "library absent" branch structurally unreachable rather than merely better-reported. **The measured fact that makes this closure real, and not a restatement of intent:** a conflicting grab now reports `BadAccess`/`X_GrabKey` **synchronously**, read through `XSetErrorHandler` + `XSync` inside `_takeGrab`, and every state successfully taken in the batch is ungrabbed so nothing is left half-held — where the removed plugin discarded `keybinder_bind`'s `gboolean` and answered `fl_method_success_response_new(fl_value_new_bool(true))` regardless (`hotkey_manager_linux-0.2.0/linux/hotkey_manager_linux_plugin.cc:96`, `:99-100`). Probed live against a private `Xvfb :77` on 2026-09-01: with a `ctypes` client holding Ctrl+Shift+G, `X11KeyGrabRegistrar.grab()` answered REFUSED with the sentence the adapter turns into `HotkeyUnavailable`; after the holder released, the same call answered HELD; release-then-re-grab answered HELD with no double-hold. So AD-10's "a failed grab must not report success" is satisfiable through this seam, which is this entry's whole subject. **Two of the four entries this entry's `decision:` names as closing with it were already closed and were NOT re-touched by this phase:** DW-42 reads `status: done 2026-08-14` and DW-43 reads `status: done 2026-08-14`, both closed before Phase 1 began; the append-only rule covers closed entries too, so their dates and resolutions are untouched. For the record, both closures survive the route change intact: DW-42 closed on the plugin leaving the process, which is exactly what happened; DW-43 closed on the seven labels no longer being bindable, and plan 01-07 went further — measured three ways on 2026-09-01, the divergence was an artefact of the vendor package's `kGtkToLogicalKey` reverse scan, not of X11, keybinder or GTK, so all seven now bind **correctly** through `XStringToKeysym` (`lib/src/infrastructure/hotkey/hotkey_key_catalogue.dart:120-146`). Still owed and NOT closed here: DW-9's real-desktop checklist. The refusal is proved at the registrar layer on `Xvfb` with synthetic clients; that the settings screen renders it as unavailable and names the tray needs a GUI session with a StatusNotifier host, which this container does not have (`.planning/phases/01-hotkey-truth/01-02-SUMMARY.md`, coverage id D1 — recorded there as owed, not passed).
decision: 2026-08-14 Build the `dart:ffi` keybinder registrar — engineering call, and a dependency choice AD-9 and AGENTS.md §4.2 both confine to one file. Verified in the pinned package: `hotkey_manager_linux-0.2.0/linux/hotkey_manager_linux_plugin.cc:96` calls `keybinder_bind(keystring, handle_key_down, NULL)` and discards its `gboolean`, then answers `fl_method_success_response_new(fl_value_new_bool(true))` unconditionally at :99-100 — no Dart code can tell a taken grab from a refused one. `hotkey_manager_registrar.dart` is replaced by a `keybinder_registrar.dart` that opens `libkeybinder-3.0.so.0` with `DynamicLibrary.open` and calls `keybinder_init`/`keybinder_bind` itself, marshalled onto the GTK main thread with `g_idle_add` and answered through a `NativeCallable.listener`; `X11GlobalHotkey` and everything above it are untouched. This is the single change that closes four entries: this one (a refusal becomes readable, so AD-10's "a failed grab must not report success" becomes satisfiable), DW-40 (a missing library becomes a caught error instead of a loader failure before `main()`), DW-42 (the vendor's undefined behaviour leaves the process) and DW-43 (the keyval is computed here, so the seven shadowed keys come back). Its threading surface is untestable in this container and is owed a real X session — itemised on DW-9's checklist.

### DW-40: a missing `libkeybinder-3.0.so.0` stops the daemon starting, which contradicts the spine's "never blocks startup"

origin: story 7-x11-global-hotkey-adapter.md, 2026-08-08
location: `hotkey_manager_linux-0.2.0/linux/CMakeLists.txt:45-54`, linux/flutter/generated_plugins.cmake, ARCHITECTURE-SPINE.md (Operational envelope)
severity: medium
reason: The spine's Operational envelope lists `libkeybinder-3.0-0` among the runtime dependencies the app cannot supply itself and states that each missing one "degrades one capability visibly (AD-12, AD-19) and **never blocks startup**". That is not true of this one. The Linux plugin resolves `keybinder-3.0` through `pkg_check_modules(... REQUIRED)` and a missing one is a build-time `FATAL_ERROR`; the built binary then carries it as a direct `DT_NEEDED`, verified here — `ldd build/linux/x64/debug/bundle/hotkey_grammar_corrector` reports `libkeybinder-3.0.so.0 => /lib/x86_64-linux-gnu/libkeybinder-3.0.so.0`. On a machine without it the dynamic loader fails before `main()`: nothing degrades, nothing starts, and there is no tray menu to fix it from. **Pre-existing** — the dependency has been pinned and in `generated_plugins.cmake` since before this story, which neither causes nor worsens it; what this story adds is the evidence and the reading of the envelope clause it contradicts. Closing it means either the `dart:ffi` replacement in DW-39 (where `DynamicLibrary.open` makes absence a caught error) or amending the envelope to say this dependency is hard. Note the premise the dispatch carried was also wrong in the other direction: keybinder-3.0 **is** installed in this container (0.3.2, headers and shared library), and `flutter build linux --debug` succeeds.
status: done 2026-09-02
resolution: 2026-09-02 Closed by plan 01-02 (`6abf480`), **on measurement rather than on assertion.** The envelope's "never blocks startup" clause is true of this dependency again because the dependency is gone from the link set, not because it was made to degrade. Measured on a release bundle built eight seconds before the reading (`flutter clean` + `flutter pub get` + `flutter build linux --release`; binary mtime 09:36:18, measured 09:36:26): `readelf -d <exe> | grep -c hotkey_manager` went `1` -> `0`, so the runner's own `DT_NEEDED` no longer names the plugin library; `ldd <exe> | grep -c keybinder` went `1` -> `0`; `ls build/linux/x64/release/bundle/lib/ | grep -c hotkey_manager` went `1` -> `0`, so no plugin shared object ships in the bundle at all; and `ldd` over **every** `.so` in `bundle/lib/` finds no keybinder reference anywhere. The whole chain the entry describes — runner -> `libhotkey_manager_linux_plugin.so` -> `libkeybinder-3.0.so.0`, resolved by the dynamic loader before `main()` and therefore before `DisplayServer.fromEnvironment` chooses an adapter — no longer exists, so a host without libkeybinder now starts the daemon instead of dying with nothing to surface and no tray to fix it from (`.planning/phases/01-hotkey-truth/01-02-SUMMARY.md`, coverage id D2). **This entry's `decision:` line already corrected its own `reason:`** — the executable never carried libkeybinder as a *direct* `DT_NEEDED`; the plugin library did — and that correction was **re-confirmed this phase** and mattered operationally: plan 01-02's precondition and its acceptance criterion #1 both read the executable for `keybinder` and would have printed `0` **before** the change as well, so the phase's most severe defect would have been gated by a criterion that could not distinguish success from failure. Plan 01-02 reported that criterion as vacuous and substituted the three non-vacuous measurements above, which is an alignment to this already-ratified `decision:` rather than a new gate (that plan's Deviation 1). Both texts stand; neither `reason:` nor `decision:` is rewritten. Not closed by this: the envelope prose itself still names `libkeybinder-3.0-0` at `ARCHITECTURE-SPINE.md:583-584` — the amendment is filed for Phase 7 (ARCH-06) as its own open entry appended 2026-09-02, since this phase edited no line of the spine.
decision: 2026-08-14 Closed by DW-39's replacement, and amend the envelope meanwhile — engineering call. The entry's conclusion holds and its evidence needs one correction: the executable does **not** carry libkeybinder as a direct `DT_NEEDED`. Measured here with `objdump -p build/linux/x64/release/bundle/hotkey_grammar_corrector`, its NEEDED list names `libhotkey_manager_linux_plugin.so`, and it is that library which carries `NEEDED libkeybinder-3.0.so.0`. The consequence is identical — the dynamic loader resolves it before `main()`, so a host without it starts nothing, degrades nothing and offers no tray menu to fix it from. Also verified: `hotkey_manager_linux-0.2.0/linux/CMakeLists.txt:45-54` makes a missing keybinder a build-time `FATAL_ERROR`, and keybinder 0.3.2 is installed in this container. `DynamicLibrary.open` in DW-39's registrar turns absence into a caught error and an honest `HotkeyUnavailable`; until that lands, DW-2's spine refresh states in the Operational envelope that this one dependency is hard rather than degrading, so the envelope stops claiming something untrue.

### DW-41: AD-9's `<Ctrl><Shift>g` is not the syntax the backend uses, and no Dart code produces the accelerator at all

origin: story 7-x11-global-hotkey-adapter.md, 2026-08-08
location: ARCHITECTURE-SPINE.md AD-9 vs `hotkey_manager_linux-0.2.0/linux/hotkey_manager_linux_plugin.cc:89-90`
severity: low
reason: AD-9 says each adapter "serializes `HotkeyBinding` to its own syntax (`<Ctrl><Shift>g` for keybinder …)". Two things are wrong with that as a description of what ships. First, the adapter does not build the string: `hkm_register` calls `gtk_accelerator_name(keyval, mods)` in C, from the keyval and modifier names it is sent. Second, the illustration is not what GTK produces — probed directly against GTK 3 in this container, Ctrl+Shift+G yields **`<Shift><Control>g`**: a different spelling *and* a different order, with an uppercase `GDK_KEY_G` normalising to the same string. A Dart copy of that string would be a prediction of vendor behaviour, used by nothing and free to drift into a misleading diagnostic (AGENTS.md §1), so it was deliberately not written; AD-9's *intent* is honoured exactly — `HotkeyKeyCatalogue` translates the domain type inside infrastructure and no keyval, `HotKey` or accelerator rises above `lib/src/infrastructure/hotkey/` (`test/architecture/hotkey_confinement_test.dart`). A spine currency item alongside DW-2 and DW-7, recorded rather than hand-edited into the spine, following the convention stories 1, 2 and 4 used.
status: done 2026-08-14
resolution: resolved by sweep bundle dw-spine-currency-refresh
decision: 2026-08-14 Carried by DW-2's spine refresh — engineering call. Verified both halves: ARCHITECTURE-SPINE.md:270 does say each adapter serializes to `<Ctrl><Shift>g`, and no Dart under `lib/` produces an accelerator at all — `hkm_register` builds it in C with `gtk_accelerator_name` (plugin :90), and `hotkey_key_catalogue.dart:7-11` already records the probed GTK output as `<Shift><Control>g`, a different spelling and a different order. The code's deliberate refusal to write a Dart copy is right and stays. Note this is the one currency item that changes twice: after DW-39's FFI registrar the accelerator genuinely will be built in Dart, so the refresh should state what is true then rather than what is true now.

### DW-42: `handle_key_down` and `hkm_unregister` read an uninitialised `const char*` when their map lookup misses

origin: story 7-x11-global-hotkey-adapter.md, 2026-08-08
location: `hotkey_manager_linux-0.2.0/linux/hotkey_manager_linux_plugin.cc:34-53` and `:101-119`
severity: medium
reason: Both functions declare `const char* identifier;` / `const char* keystring;`, assign it only inside `if (result != hotkey_id_map.end())`, and then use it unconditionally — `fl_value_new_string(identifier)` in the key-down handler, `keybinder_unbind(keystring, handle_key_down)` in the unregister handler. On a miss the pointer is indeterminate and both are undefined behaviour in a process designed to stay resident all day. `hotkey_id_map` is a file-scope `std::map` shared by every registration in the process, so a miss is reachable: an `unregister` for an identifier never registered (or registered and already erased) takes the second path, and a keybinder callback for a keystring the map no longer holds takes the first. This adapter narrows the exposure rather than removing it — `HotkeyManagerRegistrar` holds at most one `HotKey`, uses one stable identifier, and never unregisters what it did not register — but it cannot close it, and `hotKeyManager.unregisterAll()` (used by this project's own test teardown) walks the map by value and so is safe only while the map is consistent. A vendor defect: the realistic remedies are an upstream fix or the `dart:ffi` replacement in DW-39, which does not go through this plugin at all.
status: done 2026-08-14
decision: 2026-08-14 Close — removed by DW-39 — engineering call, contingent on that entry's build.
resolution: closed by engineering decision 2026-08-14, contingent on DW-39: the defect is real and was re-verified in the pinned package — `handle_key_down` declares `const char* identifier;` (`hotkey_manager_linux-0.2.0/linux/hotkey_manager_linux_plugin.cc:34`), assigns it only inside the map hit (:41-42) and passes it to `fl_value_new_string` unconditionally (:46), and `hkm_unregister` does the same with `const char* keystring;` (:106, :112-113, :115). Both are undefined behaviour on a map miss in a process designed to stay resident all day. It is a vendor defect this codebase can narrow but not close, and DW-39's FFI registrar does not go through this plugin at all — so once that lands the plugin is not in the process and there is nothing here to guard and no upstream patch to chase. Re-file if DW-39 is ever abandoned.

### DW-43: seven of the catalogue's sixty-three key labels reach keybinder as a keypad, ISO or 3270 keyval rather than the ordinary one

origin: story 7-x11-global-hotkey-adapter.md, 2026-08-08
location: `uni_platform-0.1.3/lib/src/extensions/keyboard_key.dart` (`ExtendedPhysicalKeyboardKey.keyCode`) vs Flutter's `kGtkToLogicalKey`; observed in test/platform/hotkey_manager_registrar_test.dart
severity: medium
reason: `hotkey_manager`'s platform interface sends `'keyCode': hotKey.physicalKey.keyCode`, and `uni_platform` resolves that on Linux by scanning Flutter's `kGtkToLogicalKey` for the **first** entry whose value is the logical key. That table lists the keypad, ISO and 3270 variants ahead of the ordinary ones, so the first match is wrong for seven of the labels this build offers. Measured through the real chain against the mocked channel: `Space` → `0xff80` (`GDK_KEY_KP_Space`, not `0x20`), `Tab` → `0xfe20` (`ISO_Left_Tab`, not `0xff09`), `Enter` → `0xfd1e` (`3270_Enter`, not `0xff0d`), and `F1`–`F4` → `0xff91`–`0xff94` (`KP_F1`–`KP_F4`, not `0xffbe`–`0xffc1`). `gtk_accelerator_name` then builds a perfectly valid keystring for a key almost no keyboard has — probed against GTK 3 here, the Alt+Space a user configures becomes `<Alt>KP_Space` where `<Alt>space` is what they press — `keybinder_bind` reports success (DW-39 says it always does), and the shortcut never fires. Everything else is correct — all 26 letters, all 10 digits, `F5`–`F12` and the other eleven named keys — including the shipped default Ctrl+Shift+G (`0x67`). Not fixed here for two reasons: the choice of USB HID usage is the only input this project controls and no alternative usage maps to the ordinary keyval, so it is unfixable through this package (the `dart:ffi` replacement in DW-39 computes the keyval itself and closes it); and narrowing the catalogue to drop the seven would contradict this story's own B4/B5 rows, which require `Space`, `Tab`, `Enter`, `F1` and `F12` to resolve. Pinned instead by an explicit row in `hotkey_manager_registrar_test.dart` asserting all seven observed values plus four correct controls, so an upstream fix or a Flutter key-table edit fails a test rather than silently changing which physical key a user's shortcut binds.
status: done 2026-08-14
decision: 2026-08-14 Close — resolved — engineering call. The defect this entry names is no longer reachable; what is left is a capability gap that DW-39 closes.
resolution: already resolved: the seven labels can no longer bind the wrong key, because they can no longer be bound at all. `HotkeyKeyCatalogue.labelsThatBindTheWrongKey` (`lib/src/infrastructure/hotkey/hotkey_key_catalogue.dart:134-142`) enumerates `Space`, `Tab`, `Enter` and `F1`-`F4`, and `x11_global_hotkey.dart:136-145` refuses them before touching the backend with a message that names why and ends in the tray clause — so no user can configure a shortcut that reports success and silently never fires, which was the whole defect. The measured keyvals stay pinned as data in `test/platform/hotkey_manager_registrar_test.dart:210-256`, so an upstream fix or a Flutter key-table edit still fails a test. The residual is a capability gap — seven of sixty-three labels unusable on X11 — and restoring them is DW-39's FFI registrar, which computes the keyval itself and never consults `kGtkToLogicalKey`.

### DW-44: no real X11 grab, CAP-1 summon or CAP-12 live rebind was ever observed

origin: story 7-x11-global-hotkey-adapter.md, 2026-08-08
location: lib/src/infrastructure/hotkey/{x11_global_hotkey,hotkey_manager_registrar}.dart, test/platform/x11_hotkey_live_test.dart
severity: medium
reason: This story ships the first code that can take a real global key grab, and **none** of its user-visible claims were observed here. Not that keybinder accepts the combination, not that a press raises the panel inside CAP-1's 100 ms budget, not that CAP-12's rebind takes effect without a restart, and — separately and unfixably in any container, per DW-39 — not whether `keybinder_bind` succeeded at all. `keybinder-3.0` grabs keys on an X display and this process can reach none: no compositor, no session, no reachable X display (`:10` answers "Authorization required, but no authorization protocol specified", there is no `~/.Xauthority`, no `XAUTHORITY`, and no `Xvfb`/`xvfb-run` to stand one up), and no way to synthesise a key press for a grab to catch. What is proven instead, by tests that do run: the bind outcome logic, the serialized-rebind order and every refusal path against a `HotkeyRegistrar` fake; the label-to-usage serialization against explicit numbers; the keyval, the modifier names, the register/unregister order, the disposed-seam refusals and the press route against the mocked `hotkey_manager` channels; and `flutter build linux --debug` with `libkeybinder-3.0.so.0` linked in. None of those is a runtime observation and none may be reported as one. `test/platform/x11_hotkey_live_test.dart` is an unconditionally skipped test with a `fail()` body naming what is owed, so it can never pass here. Distinct from DW-9 (AD-8's window is never mapped at startup) and DW-26 (CAP-1's "visible and focused" and CAP-14's focus-loss hide): those are the same missing display for a different claim, and this is the hotkey's own. Owed on a machine with a real X session before the daemon is called done.
status: done 2026-08-14
resolution: resolved by sweep bundle dw-runtime-observation-checklists
decision: 2026-08-14 Carried by DW-9's checklist, and it grows — engineering call. Verified still unobservable here: `keybinder-3.0` grabs on an X display, this container reaches none, and `test/platform/x11_hotkey_live_test.dart` remains an unconditionally skipped test with a `fail()` body so the claim can never be reported as met from here. The real grab, the CAP-1 summon inside 100 ms and the CAP-12 live rebind become itemised steps on the one checklist. What this entry gains from this sweep: DW-39's FFI registrar adds a GTK-main-thread marshalling surface that no test in any container can touch, so the checklist has to exercise that too — the two are owed on the same session.

- source_spec: `_bmad-output/specs/spec-hotkey-grammar-corrector/stories/7-x11-global-hotkey-adapter.md`
  summary: The domain's `HotkeyBinding.modifiers` is a plain mutable `Set` that participates in `==`/`hashCode` with no defensive copy, so a caller mutating the set it passed in silently changes an already-hashed value's bucket.
  evidence: Pre-existing — `lib/src/domain/hotkey/hotkey_binding.dart` has carried this since story 4 added value equality, and the equality is load-bearing for AD-10 (the settings screen compares the requested binding with the one the backend reported) and for `SettingsController._ownWrites`, which is a value-equality `Set<AppConfig>` reaching `HotkeyBinding` transitively. Story 7 fixed the identical hole in its own new `HotkeyGrab` with `Set.unmodifiable`, which is what surfaced this one; the domain type was left alone because AD-9 fixes its declaration verbatim and story 7's Never list rules out domain edits. Adding a defensive copy is a member-level change of the same class DW-7 already records, not a field change.
  status: parked 2026-08-13

- source_spec: `_bmad-output/specs/spec-hotkey-grammar-corrector/stories/7-x11-global-hotkey-adapter.md`
  summary: `test/platform/panel_visibility_live_test.dart`'s skip reason still states this container has "no keybinder-3.0", which story 7 disproved by measurement.
  evidence: `pkg-config --modversion keybinder-3.0` answers 0.3.2, the headers are at `/usr/include/keybinder-3.0/`, `libkeybinder-3.0.so.0` is in `ldconfig`, and `ldd` on the built bundle shows the daemon linking it directly. The claim entered the tree as a repetition of the dispatch premise every story since 5 has carried, and story 7's Design Notes correct it — leaving two project documents contradicting each other about what is installed here. Not patched by story 7 because the file belongs to story 5 and the dispatch forbids touching changes unrelated to the spec; the correction is a one-clause edit whenever that file is next opened. What remains true in that skip reason is the part that matters to it: there is no compositor and no reachable X display.
  status: parked 2026-08-13

- source_spec: `_bmad-output/specs/spec-hotkey-grammar-corrector/stories/7-x11-global-hotkey-adapter.md`
  summary: A hotkey configured with no modifiers and an ordinary typing key now takes a system-wide grab on that bare key, making the character untypeable anywhere and toggling the panel on every press.
  evidence: `HotkeyBinding(modifiers: {}, key: 'G')` is legal config — story 1 ratified empty modifier sets — and `HotkeyKeyCatalogue` resolves `G`, so `gtk_accelerator_name(0x67, 0)` yields `"g"` and keybinder grabs the bare key for the X session. Against the story-4 stub this was inert; story 7 is what makes it live. The spec's matrix row A12 requires an empty modifier set to be grabbed and exercises it with `F12`, where the behaviour is unobjectionable, so refusing bare printable keys would go beyond what the intent settles. It is a genuine design question with no spec basis — whether the app should refuse a shortcut the user explicitly asked for, and if so where the line between `F12` and `G` falls — and the only surface for undoing it is the same config file, on a daemon whose panel the user may no longer be able to summon. Worth settling deliberately alongside story 10's settings screen, which is the surface that would otherwise offer it.
  status: parked 2026-08-13

- source_spec: `_bmad-output/specs/spec-hotkey-grammar-corrector/stories/7-x11-global-hotkey-adapter.md`
  summary: A rebind abandoned because its release was refused resolves to `HotkeyBound` carrying the previous combination, so the settings surface shows a truthful effective binding with no signal that the change the user asked for did not happen.
  evidence: `X11GlobalHotkey._releaseBeforeRebinding` returns `HotkeyBound(effective: <still-live binding>)` when the release rejects, which is exactly AD-10's contract — `effective` is "what is actually in effect" — and story 7's Design Notes argue it against the alternative of grabbing anyway, which would leave keybinder holding two combinations. But `SettingsController` sets `SettingsFailure` only when `bind()` throws or answers `HotkeyUnavailable`, so on this path the user sees the old combination rendered as effective and no failure banner: accurate field by field, and silent about the request. The honest fix needs either a third arm on the bind outcome or a controller-side comparison of requested against effective, both of which are decisions for the surface that renders them. Related to the already-filed bind-before-write divergence and the mutation-generation race, which story 7 makes live rather than latent by putting a real grab behind `changeHotkey`.
  status: parked 2026-08-13

- source_spec: `_bmad-output/specs/spec-hotkey-grammar-corrector/stories/7-x11-global-hotkey-adapter.md`
  summary: `HotkeyModifier.meta` reaches keybinder as GDK's *virtual* `GDK_META_MASK`, not the Mod4 a Linux Super key produces, so a Super-based shortcut may bind a different mask or none at all — the modifier-axis twin of DW-43, and unobserved here.
  evidence: The seam maps `meta` to `HotKeyModifier.meta`, whose name reaches the native `get_mods` and ORs in `GDK_META_MASK` (1<<28), a virtual modifier GTK resolves through the keymap rather than a real X modifier. On the common Linux setup the Super key is Mod4 and `GDK_SUPER_MASK` is what it produces, while Meta is unmapped or aliased onto Mod1 — so `gtk_accelerator_name` yields `<Meta>g` and keybinder grabs Alt+G, or nothing, for a user who configured Super+G. This is the same failure shape as DW-43 (a grab keybinder accepts and reports as successful for a combination nobody can press, per DW-39) on the modifier axis instead of the key axis, and `labelsThatBindTheWrongKey` covers key labels only. Not settled here because it cannot be: the assertion needs a real X display and a keymap, and `test/platform/hotkey_manager_registrar_test.dart` can only observe that the string `meta` reaches the channel — the resulting mask is computed natively from a keymap this container does not have. Distinct from DW-44, which owes the *happy path* on a real desktop; this owes a specific expected-wrong result for one of the four modifiers this app offers, and if it is confirmed the answer is either to refuse `meta` the way the seven key labels are refused or to send `super` instead, neither of which an unattended run should guess at. Worth measuring first on a real session, alongside DW-44.
  status: parked 2026-08-13

- source_spec: `_bmad-output/specs/spec-hotkey-grammar-corrector/stories/8-wayland-portal-global-hotkey-adapter.md`
  summary: AD-10's "displays the effective combination read back from the portal" is unimplementable through `HotkeyRegistration` as AD-9 declares it, because the portal returns only a localized `trigger_description` and the type has nowhere to put it.
  evidence: `BindShortcuts` answers with `description` and `trigger_description` per shortcut, the latter documented as "user-readable text describing how to trigger the shortcut for the client to render" — localized, backend-specific, and not a trigger. There is no machine-readable combination anywhere in the reply, so `WaylandPortalGlobalHotkey` reports `effective: null` (AD-10's own "null when the backend cannot report it") and logs the description instead. That satisfies AD-10's *value* rule and leaves its *display* rule with nothing to render: the settings screen story is the consumer, and it needs either a third field on `HotkeyRegistration` or a second value beside it. Both are spine renegotiations — AD-9 fixes the declaration verbatim and this story's Never list rules out domain edits — so it is filed rather than guessed at. Parsing the description back into a `HotkeyBinding` was considered and declined: it would be a prediction of vendor and locale behaviour that misreports silently (AGENTS.md §1), the same reason story 7 declined to reconstruct the keybinder accelerator.
  status: parked 2026-08-13

- source_spec: `_bmad-output/specs/spec-hotkey-grammar-corrector/stories/8-wayland-portal-global-hotkey-adapter.md`
  summary: AD-11 mandates the `ShortcutsChanged` subscription but the `GlobalHotkey` port has no way to push a changed registration upward, so a rebind the user makes in the compositor's own settings updates one log line and nothing else.
  evidence: The subscription ships and is filtered on the live session handle (`wayland_portal_global_hotkey_test.dart` rows A19 and A19b), and the handler logs the new `trigger_description` at info level. There is nowhere else for it to go: `GlobalHotkey` exposes `activations`, `bind` and `dispose`, and `bind`'s return value is only produced by a call this app made. AD-10 explicitly asks for the opposite — "subscribe to `ShortcutsChanged` so a rebind made in the compositor updates the UI" — which needs a stream or a callback on the port, and therefore the same spine renegotiation as the entry above. The two are one decision and should be settled together with the settings-screen story. Until then a user who rebinds in GNOME's settings sees a stale combination in this app's settings until the next bind.
  status: parked 2026-08-13

- source_spec: `_bmad-output/specs/spec-hotkey-grammar-corrector/stories/8-wayland-portal-global-hotkey-adapter.md`
  summary: A portal that never answers a `Response` leaves `bind()` pending forever, so `_finishStartup` never reaches the tray's `setHotkeyUnavailable` and the tray keeps asserting that hotkeys are available.
  evidence: AD-11 makes a portal dialog the user must answer normal, so there is deliberately no timeout on a `Response` — cancelling a dialog out from under a user would be worse, and a user who dismisses it produces a non-zero `Response`, which is handled and tested (row A10). The narrow remaining case is a portal that answers never: `_callThroughRequest` awaits a `Completer` that only the signal completes, `DaemonStartup.bindHotkey` awaits that, and the tray's state is set after it. This is the hotkey-path instance of the timeout-policy question already filed against `tray.install()` and `DaemonLifecycle._step`, and it should be decided once for all three rather than three times. Note what it is *not*: an unhandled rejection or a crash. The daemon stays up and the tray menu still opens the panel; what is wrong is that the tray shows the wrong state while it waits.
  status: parked 2026-08-13

- source_spec: `_bmad-output/specs/spec-hotkey-grammar-corrector/stories/8-wayland-portal-global-hotkey-adapter.md`
  summary: The reverse-DNS application id is a constant in the Wayland adapter and the packaging story's `.desktop` basename must be pinned against it, or the two drift and GNOME discards the bind with nothing failing.
  evidence: AD-11 requires the app id to have an installed `.desktop` file of the *same basename*, and `WaylandPortalGlobalHotkey.applicationId` is `com.divertedriver.HotkeyGrammarCorrector` — deliberately public so a test can name it, but nothing yet ships `com.divertedriver.HotkeyGrammarCorrector.desktop` (the spine's Structural Seed puts it under `linux/packaging/`). If the two ever spell the id differently the failure is silent in the worst way: `BindShortcuts` succeeds and returns an empty `shortcuts` set, which this adapter correctly reports as unavailable naming the missing entry — so the user sees "no hotkey" and the only clue is a message, with no test failing anywhere. A one-line architecture test comparing the constant with the shipped file's basename closes it, and can only be written once the file exists.
  status: parked 2026-08-13

- source_spec: `_bmad-output/specs/spec-hotkey-grammar-corrector/stories/8-wayland-portal-global-hotkey-adapter.md`
  summary: AD-11's `Registry.Register` step is shipped unconditionally while a sandboxed (Flatpak) build must not call it, and no packaging format has been chosen.
  evidence: The spine's Deferred section says a sandboxed app must not call `Registry.Register` — inside a sandbox the portal derives the app id from the sandbox metadata and the host Registry is for un-sandboxed apps only. The non-sandboxed path ships, with the rule written on `_registerApplicationIdOnce`, and **no sandbox detector was built on purpose**: the signals one would key off include `/.dockerenv`, which story 6 established is true in this container, so a detector would take the sandboxed branch in every test run and leave the first invariant of AD-11 executed by nothing. Whoever picks a packaging format changes that one method. Worth noting what the exposure actually is today: on a sandboxed build the extra `Register` call is tolerated by the same paths that tolerate a portal with no Registry, so the likely symptom is a mis-associated app id rather than a crash — but that is unmeasured, because this container has no portal at all.
  status: parked 2026-08-13

- source_spec: `_bmad-output/specs/spec-hotkey-grammar-corrector/stories/8-wayland-portal-global-hotkey-adapter.md`
  summary: `bind()` answering `HotkeyBound` with `effective: null` gives `SettingsController` no way to tell a compositor-chosen combination from a request the compositor never honoured — the Wayland twin of the abandoned-rebind entry story 7 filed.
  evidence: Two different outcomes now produce the identical value `HotkeyBound(HotkeyRegistration(effective: null, authority: compositor))`: a bind the compositor accepted (row A1) and a rebind abandoned because `Session.Close` was refused, where the *previous* combination is what is still live (row A6). `SettingsController` raises `SettingsFailure` only when `bind()` throws or answers `HotkeyUnavailable`, so on the second path the user sees no failure banner and a settings screen that reflects the request they made rather than the one in force. This is the same shape as story 7's X11 entry and has the same two candidate fixes — a third arm on the bind outcome, or a controller-side comparison — except that here `effective` cannot supply the comparison at all, which makes it strictly worse than the X11 case. Related and deliberately left filed: the `SettingsController` bind-before-write divergence and its mutation-generation race. **This story makes both of those live on Wayland as well as X11, and the mutation-generation race is worse here**, because AD-11 puts a portal dialog the user must answer inside the window, making it seconds wide by design rather than a channel round trip.
  status: parked 2026-08-13

- source_spec: `_bmad-output/specs/spec-hotkey-grammar-corrector/stories/8-wayland-portal-global-hotkey-adapter.md`
  summary: `package:dbus` 0.7.14 never completes an in-flight method call when its socket closes, and poisons a client whose first connect throws — so a `dispose()` landing mid-handshake leaves that `bind()` pending forever, and no error can be tolerated on a connection that failed.
  evidence: Both measured here against 0.7.14. (1) `DBusClient.close()` closes the socket and leaves every entry of `_methodCalls` un-completed; a probe that parked a call in the fake service, closed the client, then released the call found the future still pending after two seconds. `dispose()` is deliberately not queued behind `bind()` (a daemon that must exit cannot wait on a portal dialog), so a stop signal during the startup bind leaves `bind()` unresolved. It is benign in the daemon — every path that sets `_disposed` is followed by `exit(0)`, and the pending future is simply abandoned — but it is why the adapter's post-await `_disposed` guards cannot be exercised by any test in this container: the awaits they follow never resume. (2) `_connect()` sets `_connectCompleter` before `await _openSocket()` and never completes it if that throws, so every later call on that client waits on a completer nobody will complete. This is the reason the adapter treats a *connection* failure at AD-11's step 1 as fatal instead of tolerating it the way AD-11 tolerates a Registry refusal: carrying on would not degrade, it would hang `bind()` for the life of the daemon and the tray would never be told (row A7 pins the answer). A vendor defect with two realistic remedies — an upstream fix that errors pending calls on close and resets the completer on a failed connect, or a wrapper here that races every call against a disposal signal. The wrapper was not built because it is a mechanism this story's intent does not ask for and its only observable benefit is a promptly-resolved future nobody awaits.
  status: parked 2026-08-13

- source_spec: `_bmad-output/specs/spec-hotkey-grammar-corrector/stories/8-wayland-portal-global-hotkey-adapter.md`
  summary: `linux/CMakeLists.txt` sets `APPLICATION_ID` to `com.divertedriver.hotkey_grammar_corrector` while AD-11 and this adapter register `com.divertedriver.HotkeyGrammarCorrector`, so the id the process announces to the session and the id the portal associates the bind with are two different strings.
  evidence: `linux/CMakeLists.txt:10` carries the snake_case value, and `linux/runner/my_application.cc:140,143` feeds it to `g_set_prgname` and the GApplication `application-id`; the adapter's `applicationId` constant is the spine's reverse-DNS form, which is also the basename the spine's Structural Seed reserves for `linux/packaging/com.divertedriver.HotkeyGrammarCorrector.desktop`. Pre-existing — the CMake value is what `flutter create` derives from the pubspec name and has been in the tree since the bootstrap — and this story is what makes it matter: AD-11 says GNOME discards a bind whose app id has no matching installed desktop entry, and a packaging story that follows `g_set_prgname` would ship the wrong basename. Not reconciled here because the choice is packaging's (rename the CMake id, or ship the desktop entry under the spine's name and accept two ids), and because changing `APPLICATION_ID` alters the GTK application identity of a daemon whose window behaviour no test in this container can observe. Story 11 owns it; the adapter's constant is now pinned by literal so at least one side cannot drift silently.
  raised_by: adversarial lens, story 8 review pass
  status: done 2026-08-11
  resolution: Closed by story 11, which took the first of the two options the entry names — the CMake id was renamed rather than the desktop entry shipped under a second name. `linux/CMakeLists.txt` now sets `APPLICATION_ID` to `com.divertedriver.HotkeyGrammarCorrector`, so `g_set_prgname`, the GApplication `application-id`, `WaylandPortalGlobalHotkey.applicationId` and the basename of the newly-shipped `linux/packaging/com.divertedriver.HotkeyGrammarCorrector.desktop` are one string. `test/architecture/desktop_entries_test.dart` pins all three sites against each other, discovering the packaging basename from the directory rather than naming it, so a rename of any one of them fails; the mutation gate that restores the snake_case value fails that row. `flutter build linux --debug` builds with the new id. The half the entry correctly flagged as unobservable here is still unobservable and is now filed as DW-87 with the rest of the desktop claims: nothing in this container watches a compositor associate the id with the installed file.

- source_spec: `_bmad-output/specs/spec-hotkey-grammar-corrector/stories/8-wayland-portal-global-hotkey-adapter.md`
  summary: Seven key labels are bindable on Wayland and refused on X11, so one config file does not mean the same thing on the two display servers — `Space`, `Tab`, `Enter` and `F1`-`F4` express fine as XDG triggers while `X11GlobalHotkey` refuses them before touching the backend.
  evidence: `HotkeyKeyCatalogue.labelsThatBindTheWrongKey` (DW-43) is refused at `x11_global_hotkey.dart:123`; `XdgShortcutTrigger` resolves all seven, and the adapter suite exercises `Alt+Space` reaching the wire as `ALT+space` with a `HotkeyBound` outcome. Both lenses that found it noted the story's own parity row invoked cross-display-server portability as its reason, which is why the row's reason was corrected this pass and a row added pinning the seven as expressible — so the divergence cannot grow silently, but it is not closed. Closing it means deciding whether the Wayland adapter should also refuse keys the X11 adapter cannot bind (denying a shortcut that works, to keep config portable) or whether the settings screen should present the per-display-server difference. That is story 10's surface and a product decision this story's intent does not carry.
  raised_by: adversarial and verification-gap lenses, story 8 review pass
  status: parked 2026-08-13

- source_spec: `_bmad-output/specs/spec-hotkey-grammar-corrector/stories/8-wayland-portal-global-hotkey-adapter.md`
  summary: A shutdown landing during a settings rebind leaves `SettingsController.changeHotkey` permanently unresolved, so the user's new preference is never written even when the compositor accepted the bind — the same `package:dbus` in-flight-call defect this story files as benign on the startup path, reached from a path where somebody is awaiting.
  evidence: `DBusClient.close()` never completes calls already in flight, and `dispose()` deliberately does not await `_queue` (a daemon that must exit cannot wait on a portal dialog). `SettingsController.changeHotkey` awaits `bind()` before it writes config, and `SettingsController._bind` has no timeout, so the write step is simply never reached. The already-filed entry assessed this as bounded because "every path that sets `_disposed` is followed by `exit(0)`", which is true of the startup path and not of a settings rebind racing SIGTERM. Not fixed here because the honest remedies are a bounded await on the queue inside `dispose()` or racing the pending call against a disposal signal, and both are the timeout-policy decision already open against `tray.install()` and `DaemonLifecycle._step`.
  raised_by: adversarial lens, story 8 review pass
  status: parked 2026-08-13

- source_spec: `_bmad-output/specs/spec-hotkey-grammar-corrector/stories/8-wayland-portal-global-hotkey-adapter.md`
  summary: A bus peer that can observe this daemon's session handle can still forge an `Activated` and raise a panel that reads the clipboard; the sender filter and the secure handle tokens added this pass raise the cost but do not make the session handle a secret.
  evidence: The review pass closed the two cheap holes — the `Activated`/`ShortcutsChanged` streams now filter on the portal's bus name, and the handle tokens come from `Random.secure()` rather than a time-seeded PRNG — and a row now proves an impostor client on the same bus reaches nobody. What remains is that the session handle travels over the bus in plain messages and appears in the daemon's own log context, so a peer that can read either (a bus with a permissive policy, an eavesdropping monitor, a shared log) can construct a signal that satisfies every filter the adapter applies. There is no further filter available at this layer: the portal's own bus name is already required, and D-Bus offers no per-signal authentication. Recorded rather than solved because the residual is a session-bus trust question rather than an adapter defect, and because nothing in this container can characterise a real bus policy.
  raised_by: adversarial lens, story 8 review pass
  status: parked 2026-08-13

- source_spec: `_bmad-output/specs/spec-hotkey-grammar-corrector/stories/8-wayland-portal-global-hotkey-adapter.md`
  summary: The house `_errorContext` convention reduces a caught error to its Dart runtime type, and `package:dbus` gives a Dart type to only a handful of standard D-Bus errors — so every `org.freedesktop.portal.Error.*` reaches the log as the same `DBusMethodResponseException`, and the one line the Wayland adapter's unclassified path emits carries nothing that identifies the failure.
  evidence: `_errorContext` returns `{'error_type': error.runtimeType.toString()}`, and the file's own comment calls the unclassified branch "the one case where the error type is the only clue there is". Measured against `package:dbus` 0.7.14: `DBusMethodResponseException` exposes `errorName` (`dbus_method_response.dart:35`) — a dotted protocol identifier, not payload; the payload the `Logger` port rules out is `response.values`, which is what `toString()` carries. Only errors with their own subclass (`DBusAccessDeniedException`, `DBusFailedException`, the four "no such thing" names) survive the reduction with any information, which is why row A4 reads as informative and does not generalise: it happens to pick one of the typed ones. Not fixed in this story because the shape of the error context is fixed by the intent's own Consistency Conventions ("a caught error reduced to `error_type` and never its `toString()`"), and `_errorContext` is duplicated across adapters, so adding an `error_name` field is a codebase-wide convention change rather than a patch to this file. Whoever makes it should decide it once for every adapter that reduces a vendor error.
  raised_by: adversarial lens, story 8 follow-up review pass
  status: parked 2026-08-13

- source_spec: `_bmad-output/specs/spec-hotkey-grammar-corrector/stories/8-wayland-portal-global-hotkey-adapter.md`
  summary: `Registry.Register` is latched for the life of the adapter, but the app-id association it makes lives in the `xdg-desktop-portal` process — so a portal restart plausibly leaves the new portal never told this app's id, and every later bind is discarded with a message blaming a missing `.desktop` file the user may already have installed.
  evidence: `_registered` is latched once a reply arrives and is never reset; `_registerApplicationIdOnce` returns immediately on every later bind. The restart path itself is now real rather than theoretical — the same review pass fixed a stale portal bus name that parked every bind after a restart, and added a row that restarts the fake portal and asserts the rebind completes — so the association question is reachable in a way it previously was not. Two things stop it being fixed here. It is a claim about what a real `xdg-desktop-portal` does across a restart, and this container has no portal, no compositor and no session bus, so it cannot be observed: shipping a re-registration on the strength of an inference is the vendor-behaviour prediction AGENTS.md §1 rules out. And the intent's AD-11 line says `Register` is "issued **once per process** and never re-issued on a rebind", which the remedy would contradict — re-issuing needs that decision reopened, not worked around. Whoever has a real portal should check whether the association survives a restart before either side is built.
  raised_by: edge-case lens, story 8 follow-up review pass
  status: parked 2026-08-13

- source_spec: `_bmad-output/specs/spec-hotkey-grammar-corrector/stories/8-wayland-portal-global-hotkey-adapter.md`
  summary: The dead-connection latch is permanent and has no clearing path, so a daemon that started before the session bus was up — or that outlived a `dbus-daemon` restart — answers the same failure sentence for the rest of its life, and the settings screen, the app's only retry affordance, cannot recover it.
  evidence: `_deadConnection` is written by `_recordDeadConnection` and read by `_bind`'s short circuit; there is no assignment back to `null` anywhere in the file, and `bind()` now answers from it ahead of the queue. That is deliberate and load-bearing: it works around a measured `package:dbus` 0.7.14 defect where `_connect()` assigns its completer before awaiting the socket and never completes it if that throws, so without the latch the second `bind()` on a dead client never resolves at all. The cost is that recovery needs a new `DBusClient`, which the adapter does not own the lifecycle of — it takes one by injection and closes it in `dispose()` — so clearing the latch honestly means rebuilding the client on retry, which changes the constructor's contract and the composition root's one call site. The clean remedy is upstream, and is the already-filed `package:dbus` fix (complete pending calls on close, reset the completer on a failed connect); this entry records that until it lands, "one dead connection ends hotkeys for the process" is the shipped behaviour rather than an oversight.
  raised_by: adversarial lens, story 8 follow-up review pass
  status: parked 2026-08-13

- source_spec: `_bmad-output/specs/spec-hotkey-grammar-corrector/stories/8-wayland-portal-global-hotkey-adapter.md`
  summary: This story's first acceptance criterion is written end to end — `DaemonStartup.bindHotkey` on a Wayland session producing `HotkeyBound` and telling the tray hotkeys are available — and no test asserts it, because `_hotkeyFor`'s Wayland arm has no seam to point at a fake bus.
  evidence: `daemon_startup.dart` constructs `WaylandPortalGlobalHotkey(logger: logger)` with no `DBusClient` injection point, while the X11 arm takes a `HotkeyRegistrar`. The adapter itself has a `client:` seam and the suite has a fake portal on a real bus, so both halves exist and only the composition-root wiring between them is missing. The two AD-12 bind rows that used to run on the Wayland branch were deliberately moved to the X11 arm during implementation, on the correct ground that the Wayland arm's default client would reach a developer's real session bus and open a portal dialog; what replaced them drives `requestBinding` against a socket that cannot exist, which is the unavailable half only. So the criterion is covered as two separate claims — `bindHotkey` is display-server agnostic and its tray call is pinned through the X11 arm, and the Wayland wire order and outcome are pinned at the adapter — and never as the composition. Not closed here because the intent's Code Map says `daemon_startup.dart` is "otherwise unchanged", so adding a client seam to the composition root is a scope decision rather than a patch.
  raised_by: intent-alignment lens, story 8 follow-up review pass
  status: parked 2026-08-13

### DW-46: full-suite flakes are not retired by `concurrency: 1`, contradicting the note that says they are

origin: story 9-correction-panel-ui.md, 2026-08-10
location: dart_test.yaml (`concurrency: 1`), test/infrastructure/hotkey/wayland_portal_global_hotkey_test.dart, test/infrastructure/system/single_instance_lock_test.dart
severity: medium
reason: The DW-15 entry ends "it also retires the two full-suite concurrency flakes already filed against this ledger". Observed on story 9's tree, with `concurrency: 1` in force and no story-9 change anywhere near either file: three consecutive runs of the scoped binding-free command produced a failure in run 1 (`wayland_portal_global_hotkey_test.dart` — "CAP-1: an Activated forged by another peer on the same bus reaches nobody"), a different failure in run 2 (`single_instance_lock_test.dart` — "AD-14: release frees the address for the very lock that held it"), and a clean 625 pass / 2 skip in run 3. `wayland_portal_global_hotkey_test.dart` passed 3 of 3 in isolation immediately afterwards. So serialising suites removed the overlap but not the flake: both files bind process-scoped resources (an in-process `DBusServer` on a unix path; an abstract-namespace socket) whose release is not synchronous with suite teardown, and the next suite in the same process can start before the kernel has freed them. Two consequences worth stating. First, the retirement claim in DW-15 is false as written and should not be relied on; DW-15 has been given a `seen-again:` line pointing here rather than being rewritten. Second — the operational half the ledger already worried about in the "second full-suite concurrency flake" entry — any mutation-verification claim drawn from a single full-suite run is unsound, because a false red reads as a killed mutation. Story 9's verification therefore runs mutation checks against the scoped `flutter test test/ui`, which touches neither resource. The fix is a teardown that waits for the resource to actually be released (or collapsing both socket-binding suites into one file), not more serialisation.
status: open
decision: 2026-08-14 Fix the shape, not the symptom — engineering call, with this sweep's own measurement recorded so the ledger stops carrying a stale fact as a live one. Measured today on this tree: three consecutive runs of the scoped binding-free command (`dart test --exclude-tags=live test/application test/architecture test/domain test/infrastructure test/fakes_smoke_test.dart`) each finished 779 pass / 2 skip, clean. So the entry's own 1-in-3 observation does not reproduce here — which does not disprove a flake, but does mean nobody should quote its failure rate. The structural cause it names is still present and still verified: `single_instance_lock.dart:103` binds a process-scoped abstract-namespace socket and the portal suite stands up an in-process `DBusServer`, and neither release is synchronous with suite teardown. The fix is a teardown that waits for the resource to be freed, or collapsing both socket-binding suites into one file; more serialisation is explicitly not it, and `concurrency: 1` stays for the reason `dart_test.yaml` gives rather than for this one. The operational half stands and is the durable part: no mutation-verification claim may rest on a single full-suite run, because a false red reads as a killed mutation.

### DW-48: DW-30 decided — a `restore`-sourced `true` still re-seeds, and the panel keeps that

origin: story 9-correction-panel-ui.md, 2026-08-11
location: lib/src/ui/panel/correction_panel.dart, lib/src/infrastructure/panel/window_manager_panel_visibility.dart (`_onWindowEvent`, `restore` arm)
severity: medium
reason: DW-30 asked that this be decided deliberately "when story 9 builds the panel widget", and the decision is: **unchanged behaviour**. A user who types into the panel, iconifies it and restores it still gets their text replaced by the current clipboard. AD-18 says *every* `show()` starts a fresh session re-seeded from the current clipboard, and the panel cannot tell a restore-sourced `true` from a hotkey-sourced one without a distinction `PanelVisibility` does not carry — widening the port is a domain renegotiation this story's Block If rules out, and inventing a widget-side heuristic (for example "keep the text if the user has typed") would put a second, contradictory re-seed rule in the ui ring. So nothing here narrows it, and the panel renders whatever session the controller starts. What is new since DW-30 was filed is only that the panel now makes the loss *visible* to a user rather than theoretical. Closing it needs the port to distinguish "the window came back" from "a new session starts"; the entry stays open for that.
status: done 2026-08-14
resolution: skipped by the 2026-08-14 sweep triage: Duplicate of DW-30. This entry is story 9's record that DW-30's question was answered 'unchanged behaviour', and the live question -- whether a restore-sourced `true` should re-seed the editor from the clipboard -- is triaged as a decision on DW-30. Both name the same missing port distinction between 'the window came back' and 'a new session starts' (panel_visibility.dart:12 exposes only `Stream<bool> changes`); nothing separate is owed here.

### DW-49: DW-35 is closed by construction for the panel, and stays open for its next reader

origin: story 9-correction-panel-ui.md, 2026-08-11
location: lib/src/ui/panel/correction_panel.dart, lib/src/domain/panel/panel_visibility.dart
severity: low
reason: DW-35 predicted that "story 9's panel widget will await `show()` to know when to render, and the future cannot tell it". It does not. The widget tree is always built (AD-8) and the window is mapped and unmapped underneath it, so the panel has no render decision keyed to a `show()` future — it renders `CorrectionController`'s state, and the controller is driven by `changes`, not by the future. `CorrectionPanel` never names `show()` or `hide()` at all, which `test/architecture/ad1_import_rule_test.dart`'s new ui rule and the story's `grep -rn "infrastructure" lib/src/ui/` check jointly keep true. The contract question DW-35 raises — that the future means "the intent was accepted", not "the window moved" — is untouched and still needs the domain edit it names, so the entry is left open for whoever does await it (the tray's open-panel path and `DaemonLifecycle.onShowRequest` both do).
status: done 2026-08-14
resolution: skipped by the 2026-08-14 sweep triage: Superseded by DW-35. The entry records that DW-35's prediction did not come true, and that is verified: correction_panel.dart names neither `show()` nor `hide()`, and the panel renders controller state driven by `changes` rather than by any future. The contract question it explicitly leaves open belongs to DW-35 and is carried in the panel-visibility-event-reconciliation bundle.

### DW-50: the panel window has no geometry, and nothing in this container can choose one

origin: story 9-correction-panel-ui.md, 2026-08-11
location: lib/main.dart (`_createHiddenWindow`), test/architecture/hidden_window_test.dart (the `window_manager` allowlist), lib/src/ui/panel/correction_panel.dart
severity: medium
reason: The panel now lays out against whatever size the toplevel happens to have. Nothing sets a size, a minimum size or a position: `_createHiddenWindow` calls only `setTitle` and `setSkipTaskbar`, and `hidden_window_test.dart` allowlists the startup path's `window_manager` calls, so adding `setSize`/`setMinimumSize`/`setAlignment` has to be justified there first — which this story's Never list forbids doing unattended. The consequence is that CAP-10's "readable at the same time" is verified only at a surface the *test* chose (480×360, `correction_panel_layout_test.dart`), and the real window could be 200 px tall with the first variant clipped to nothing, or full-screen with a tiny editor. This container has no compositor and no reachable X display (DW-9, DW-26), so no size can be chosen on evidence here — picking one needs someone who can see the panel. A minimum size is the likely fix, and it belongs with whoever owns the window's presentation (the settings/packaging stories).
status: done 2026-09-26
resolution: Plan 02-05 sets size, text-scale-aware minimum size, and best-effort startup position in `lib/main.dart`, clamps geometry to the startup display, and updates `test/architecture/hidden_window_test.dart`'s allowlist. D-16 is an owner-approved best-effort placement waiver: exact centering on the display currently containing the pointer is unproven, later pointer movement does not reposition the panel, and ordinary Wayland toplevel placement belongs to the compositor. No live X11 or Wayland placement was observed; strict pointer-display centering is not claimed.
decision: 2026-08-14 Fixed size, remembered position, and a conditional taskbar entry — the human scoped past the three offered options. Verified first: nothing anywhere sets a size, a minimum size or a position — `main.dart:419-424` calls only `ensureInitialized`, `setTitle` and `setSkipTaskbar(true)`. The answer is three rules. (a) **The panel is the same size on every summon** — one size, not restored, so the surface CAP-10 is measured against is the surface the user gets. (b) **It reappears where the user last left it** — position persists to the config file through the same edit-and-apply flow every other setting uses, which CAP-8 and CAP-12 require of anything user-changeable. (c) **A minimum size** equal to `CorrectionPanel.minimumPanelHeightFor` at the current text scale, so CAP-10 cannot be dragged away — this is what closes DW-53 and DW-61. And a fourth rule the human added that is not geometry: **the taskbar entry becomes conditional** — visible while the panel is showing, absent while the app is in the tray. That is load-bearing rather than cosmetic, because the human's DW-30 answer distinguishes a minimise to the *task bar* from a minimise to the *tray icon*, and that distinction presupposes a taskbar entry exists to minimise to. Consequences to carry into the build: `main.dart:423`'s unconditional `setSkipTaskbar(true)` becomes a startup default that the visibility adapter then drives on every transition, and `test/architecture/hidden_window_test.dart`'s allowlist must be extended to admit `setSize`, `setMinimumSize`, `setPosition` and `setSkipTaskbar` on the adapter — which is exactly the deliberate justification that allowlist exists to force. The chosen size and the first-run position are owed a look on a real desktop (DW-9's checklist).

location: test/ui/, dart_test.yaml
severity: low
reason: The 28 widget rows this story adds need a Flutter binding, so they run under `flutter test` and are outside the scoped binding-free command the spec verifies with (`dart test --exclude-tags=live test/application test/architecture test/domain test/infrastructure test/fakes_smoke_test.dart`). That is the same shape as DW-29, filed for `test/platform/`, and the same mitigation applies — `flutter test` is also in the gate, so nothing is unrun today — but the directory is named in no config, and every proof of CAP-2's re-seed reaching a widget, CAP-4's digit keys, CAP-11's copy button and CAP-13's inline error lives only there. Whoever adds the CI the ledger has wanted since story 2 should make `flutter test` mandatory rather than adding `test/ui` to the `dart test` list, which cannot load it.

decision: 2026-08-14 Close — subsumed by DW-29 — engineering call, contingent on that entry's build.
resolution: closed by engineering decision 2026-08-14, contingent on DW-29: `test/ui/` is verified still outside the binding-free command and still named in no config, and `.github/workflows/ci.yml` — which did not exist when this was filed — deliberately excludes `flutter test` (ci.yml:16-19), so the CI gate runs none of it. DW-29's answer adds a `flutter test` job to that workflow, which is the only command that can load these rows; this entry's own conclusion, that adding `test/ui` to the `dart test` list cannot work because it cannot load a binding, is what rules the alternative out. ### DW-52: a variant can only be selected from the keyboard; a pointer user has no way to highlight one

origin: story 9-correction-panel-ui.md, 2026-08-11

severity: low
reason: CAP-4 says "pressing 1, 2, or 3 selects the corresponding variant" and that is exactly what shipped, so this is not a breach — but the card has no tap-to-select and the digit shortcuts are scoped to the suggestions region, so a user working with the mouse can copy any variant and highlight none. Deliberately not invented here: making the card tappable is a second selection affordance the SPEC does not ask for, and the spine's Deferred section hands panel interaction detail to the widgets rather than fixing it, so adding one is a product choice rather than an omission to patch. Worth deciding alongside the panel's visual design.
